### B-075 — REQ and REP
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 408ee8e | needs: [B-074]
acceptance: REQ's strict alternation with `EFSM` on any other order, the empty delimiter prepended and stripped, round-robin out and last-peer in, REP discarding a reply whose originator vanished, `ZMQ_REQ_CORRELATE` and `ZMQ_REQ_RELAXED`; close-and-reopen after `EFSM` works, which is what Lazy Pirate needs.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b075-zmq-reqrep` by the parallel worker, off the B-074 tip 236cfc6.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 408ee8e and gated here: **603 tests**, one ignored. One commit (64d6a5a), only `crates/zmq/weida-zmq/*`. The slice is two files: `socket.rs` with `SocketCore` — round-robin send honouring the mute action, fair-queued recv, `recv_from` for the last-peer rule, `send_to` for a named peer, `within` carrying `ZMQ_SNDTIMEO`/`ZMQ_RCVTIMEO` — plus a `socket_endpoints!` macro so every socket type gets one bind/connect/unbind/disconnect/last_endpoint/close surface rather than eleven hand-written copies; and `reqrep.rs` with `ReqSocket` and `RepSocket`. `SocketCore::new` always builds the `ZmtpSession` for its socket type, so B-073's "no socket without a session" is now enforced by the only constructor. "SHALL block when it has no connected peers" became a wait rather than a poll: `Queue` gained `has_room`/`wait_for_room`/`wait_for_message` and the engine a `peers_changed` signal.
note: **REP is the first consumer of the gap B-072 left on purpose.** `zmq_socket(3)` leaves REP's "action in mute state" row blank, so `MuteAction::sending` returns `None` for it, and the answer comes from 28/REQREP instead — "SHALL not block on sending", "SHALL silently discard the reply if the originating peer is no longer connected". REP's outgoing queue therefore drops, and `RepSocket::send` returns `Sent::Dropped`, which is libzmq's silent discard **made visible as a return value** (`reqrep.rs:356-379`). That is the shape B-072 was built for: the table decides, and where the table is silent the protocol RFC is quoted at the one place the decision is taken.
note: two judgement calls worth your attention, both stated in the code rather than buried. `ZMQ_REQ_RELAXED` without `ZMQ_REQ_CORRELATE` is **refused with `EINVAL`** where libzmq documents the hazard and allows it: without correlation a late reply to an abandoned request is returned as the answer to the request that superseded it, which is silent data corruption, so this is a deliberate deviation named in the option's own documentation (0013 §4.4 item 4's "honoured or refused, never silently ignored" read strictly). And the `!Sync` half of libzmq's thread rule is carried by `PhantomData<Cell<()>>` in `SocketCore` but is **not** asserted by a test, because there is no negative bound to write and the crate has no compile-fail harness; `socket.rs:472-476` says the test checks only the `Send` half rather than pretending otherwise, which is the right way to leave a gap. Filed as B-102.

### B-102 — A compile-fail test for the socket thread rule
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done 1c927df | needs: [B-075]
acceptance: `weida-zmq`'s sockets are proved `Send` **and not** `Sync` by the test suite rather than by a `PhantomData` a refactor could delete unnoticed: a compile-fail harness (`trybuild`, dev-dependency only) asserts that sharing a socket across threads does not compile, with the expected error committed beside it, and `socket.rs:472-476`'s honest disclaimer is replaced by a reference to it. The harness covers every public socket type, so a new one cannot arrive without the check.
note: the worker's own follow-up from B-075, and it earns its place by the "fails on a plausible bug" standard: the plausible bug is someone adding an `unsafe impl Sync` or a field that makes the marker moot, which the current suite cannot see — libzmq's §2 says sharing a socket between threads "results in undefined behaviour", so this is the one invariant in the crate that only the type system can hold.
note: B-077 turned the item from a nicety into a load-bearing one, and narrowed what the harness must pin. A socket that is `!Sync` **cannot be used inside a spawned task if any method in the chain awaits behind `&self`**, because `&T: Send` requires `T: Sync` and the whole future stops being `Send`; a PUSH test that spawns a send caught it at compile time. The fix is structural — the two wait helpers are free functions over `&Engine`, which *is* `Sync`, and the timeout wrapper is a free function over `&Exec` — so every socket *future* is `Send` while the socket itself stays `!Sync`, which is libzmq's rule exactly: move a socket to another thread and use it there, never share it between two. So the harness owes two assertions, not one: sharing a socket does not compile, **and** a spawned future that owns one does.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 1c927df and gated here: **637 tests**, one ignored. One commit (8264e72), branched from a8c0562 and taken **before** B-080 although B-080 was the item marked delegated — the worker's own ordering call, and the right one: the guard it adds constrains every socket type, so landing it before another transport arrives is cheaper than after. `trybuild` is a dev-dependency only; `tests/thread_rule.rs` drives a `compile_fail` case that asks for `Sync` on every public socket type through `fn share_between_threads<T: Sync>(&T)` and a `pass` case that hands each socket type to a `tokio::spawn`ed future owning it — the two assertions B-077 showed were both needed. `socket.rs`'s disclaimer is now a pointer to the harness.
note: **the enumeration is a compile-time fact rather than a comment, and that is the part that will still be working in a year.** `for_each_socket_type!` names the eleven types once and is invoked by both trybuild files, and `socket_endpoints!` static-asserts `$socket: Enumerated` — a trait implemented only for the types that macro lists. A socket type that arrives without joining the list gets no `bind`/`connect`/`unbind`/`disconnect`/`last_endpoint`/`close` and does not compile, so the harness cannot silently fall behind the crate. A structural consequence came free: `PairSocket` used to hand-write that whole surface (its `connect` is its own — "at most one peer", `EINVAL` on the second call), which put it *outside* the macro and would have put it outside the guard; the macro grew a `no_connect` arm, `pair.rs` keeps only its own `connect`, 30 duplicated lines went away and PAIR is inside the guard. The worker verified both directions before committing rather than assuming them: changing the compile-fail file's bound from `Sync` to `Send` turns the test red, and removing `PairSocket` from the list fails the crate with "the trait bound `PairSocket: Enumerated` is not satisfied".
note: the caveat this instrument carries, recorded so that a red gate is read correctly rather than panicked over: the expected stderr is **384 lines of rustc diagnostics committed to the tree**, produced by `rustc 1.98.0 (88d9e12ae 2026-08-18)`. A toolchain upgrade that rewords a diagnostic turns this test red for a reason that has nothing to do with the invariant; the fix in that case is `TRYBUILD=overwrite cargo test -p weida-zmq --test thread_rule` followed by *reading the diff* to confirm the refusal is still a refusal, not a regeneration on autopilot. The two text-independent halves — the `pass` case and the `Enumerated` guard — carry most of the value and cannot break that way.

### B-076 — DEALER and ROUTER
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done e76af9f | needs: [B-075]
acceptance: unrestricted send and receive both ways; ROUTER's routing-id frame prepended and stripped with a peer-chosen `Identity` honoured; `ZMQ_ROUTER_MANDATORY` turning an unroutable message into `EHOSTUNREACH` instead of a silent drop; `ZMQ_ROUTER_HANDOVER` evicting an incumbent where the default rejects the collision; `ZMQ_PROBE_ROUTER`.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b076-zmq-dealer-router` by the parallel worker, off the bounds tip e2d353a. It is the first socket type that keys a table by a peer-chosen `Identity`, which B-074 parsed and deliberately left unused — and the first that should state its own ceiling as it does so, since `max_peers` bounds the peers but a routing-id table is a second map a stranger fills.
note: merged `--no-ff` as e76af9f and gated here: **610 tests**, one ignored; `weida-zmq`'s lib suite is 87, counted from the gate's own output this time. One commit (68e0e75), only `crates/zmq/weida-zmq/*`. `DealerSocket` is unrestricted both ways, round-robin over peers with room, blocking rather than discarding, and carries **no envelope** — talking to a REP means the application writes the empty delimiter, which is the honest reading of 28/REQREP. `RouterSocket` prepends the routing-id frame inbound and strips it outbound, with `ZMQ_ROUTER_MANDATORY` turning the silent drop into `EHOSTUNREACH` and a full queue into `EAGAIN` under `ZMQ_DONTWAIT` or a bounded wait otherwise, plus `ZMQ_ROUTER_HANDOVER` and `ZMQ_PROBE_ROUTER`. Supporting: `RoutingKey` beside `RoutingId` — announced versus generated, told apart by libzmq's leading-zero convention, which is what let `RoutingId`'s "first octet nonzero" rule stay intact; `AnnouncedIdentity` on the peer, filled by the session from `READY`; `Engine::evict` for the handover; and `SocketOptions::validate_for(socket_type)`, so an option a socket type cannot honour is refused at construction rather than ignored at runtime.
note: **the routing-table ceiling asked for above turned out to be derivable rather than a new option, and that is the better answer.** The table holds exactly one entry per *live* peer, because `recv`, `send` and `peers()` all reconcile it against the engine's peer set first — inserting for an arrival, dropping the entry of a departure — so the ceiling is `max_peers` entries of at most `MAX_ROUTING_ID_BYTES` peer-chosen bytes: 1024 × 255 B at the defaults, twice over for the two directions of the map. The arithmetic is stated on `RouterSocket` (`dealerrouter.rs:169-176`) rather than left to be inferred from B-099, and the shrinking half — the half a stranger would otherwise exploit by reconnecting under a new identity each time — has its own test at `dealerrouter.rs:848-852`: three DEALERs connect under three identities, are dropped one at a time, and both maps end empty. A stranger chooses the contents of a key, never how many there are.
note: two findings the worker got from a failing test rather than from reading, and both are about *when* a peer becomes knowable. A peer exists from the moment its connection is accepted, which is **before** its `READY` has been read, so the first version keyed every peer by a generated id and no announced `Identity` was ever honoured; `AnnouncedIdentity` therefore carries an announced flag and ROUTER skips a peer until its handshake has happened — which is also libzmq's own behaviour, "learns an identity only after that peer has sent something". And `ZMQ_PROBE_ROUTER` is sent by the **session** right after the handshake rather than by the socket on first use, because "a new connection" is a thing only the session sees: a socket-level probe would never fire for a socket that only ever receives, which is exactly the ROUTER case the option exists for.

### B-077 — PUSH, PULL and PAIR
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done fd11feb | needs: [B-074]
acceptance: PUSH round-robins only to peers whose queue is not full and blocks rather than discarding, PULL fair-queues its peers, and PAIR accepts at most one peer, does not auto-reconnect, and terminates further incoming connections while one is live.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b077-zmq-push-pull-pair` by the parallel worker, off the B-076 tip 68e0e75.
note: merged `--no-ff` as fd11feb and gated here: **617 tests**, one ignored; the crate's lib suite is 94, off the gate's output. One commit (45e1973), only `crates/zmq/weida-zmq/*`. `pipeline.rs` has `PushSocket` (round-robin over peers with room, blocks and never discards, `ZMQ_SNDTIMEO`) and `PullSocket` (fair-queued, `ZMQ_RCVTIMEO`). `pair.rs` has `PairSocket` with **its own endpoint surface instead of the shared macro**, because two of PAIR's rules are endpoint rules rather than message rules: `max_peers` is forced to 1, so a further incoming connection is terminated where it is accepted, and `ZMQ_RECONNECT_IVL` is forced off, so it does not auto-reconnect — both documented as properties of the type, the way every socket type's mute action is. A second `connect` is refused with `EINVAL`: one peer means one endpoint, and a PAIR dialling two is outside 31/EXPAIR altogether.
note: the tests are one per acceptance clause and two are worth naming: PULL's first two messages come from two *different* senders with both queues full, so the rotation being asserted is the sink's and not the network's accident; and a PUSH with no worker at all waits, with the task surviving to be delivered once a worker appears — "block rather than discard" and "block when there are no peers" proved in one assertion rather than two that could each pass for the wrong reason. Nine tasks over three workers come out three each, after waiting for all three to arrive, which is the pattern's own slow-joiner problem rather than a fudge in the test.
note: **the finding that will shape B-078 and B-079: a `!Sync` socket cannot be used in a spawned task if any method awaits behind `&self`**, since `&T: Send` needs `T: Sync` and the future then is not `Send`. Caught at compile time by the PUSH test that spawns a send, and fixed structurally rather than at the call site — the wait helpers became free functions over `&Engine` (which is `Sync`) and the timeout wrapper a free function over `&Exec`, so socket futures are `Send` while sockets stay `!Sync`. That is libzmq's rule precisely, and it is now a property of `SocketCore`'s shape instead of a thing each socket type must avoid tripping over. Recorded in B-102, which now owes two assertions. Smaller sibling: `PairSocket::has_peer` asks about the *connection* rather than the peer entry, because a dialled endpoint keeps its queue across a loss by the engine's design (B-073) — the first version read the entry and claimed a peer that had gone.

### B-078 — PUB and SUB
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 9c9b87f | needs: [B-074]
acceptance: publisher-side binary prefix match against the start of the first frame; subscriptions additive and non-idempotent, so two SUBSCRIBEs need two CANCELs; an empty subscription matches everything and a fresh SUB nothing; drop-not-block at the HWM; both wire forms accepted — the 3.x commands and ZMTP 2.0's one-frame `%x01`/`%x00` — with the sent form configurable.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b078-zmq-pub-sub` by the parallel worker, off the B-077 tip 45e1973. The subscription table is the third map a stranger fills, after the peers of B-099 and ROUTER's routing ids of B-076: ZMTP subscriptions are additive and non-idempotent by design, "N repeated SUBSCRIBE commands cost N entries", which is exactly what B-054 had to bound in the bridge — so the ceiling belongs in this item, stated where it is taken, not in a later one.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 9c9b87f and gated here: **627 tests**, one ignored; the crate's lib suite is 104. One commit (dd585a4), only `crates/zmq/weida-zmq/*`. `subscriptions.rs` is a bounded, reference-counted multiset of byte prefixes (`matches`/`subscribe`/`cancel`/`prefixes`/`counted`) with `SubscriptionForm` and the `%x01`/`%x00` message-form reader and writer; `pubsub.rs` has `PubSocket` — `publish` returning `Published { delivered, dropped, unmatched }` — and `SubSocket`. Engine peers and `Connection` carry the table, and `SocketCore::with_session` lets a socket type hand in its own `ZmtpSession`, which is how SUB's set reaches the handshake. The ceiling asked for above is there: `max_subscriptions`, default 1024, documented as **not** a libzmq option with the reason and the product (1024 prefixes per peer times `max_peers`), and `validate` refuses zero.
note: three shapes worth keeping. **The wire-form choice lives in exactly one place:** a SUB socket always enqueues its subscription in the `%x01`/`%x00` API form `zmq_socket(3)` describes, and the *session* turns it into a `SUBSCRIBE` command or leaves it as a message per `SubscriptionForm` — so no socket writes a command itself, and receiving accepts both forms unconditionally, which is the asymmetry the interop findings of B-043 argued for. **Re-subscription on reconnect needed no reconnect logic at all:** the socket's set is shared with its `ZmtpSession` and a session sends what it finds at its own handshake, so a reconnect re-subscribes because a reconnect *is* a new session — B-079's XSUB inherits that whole path. And the match is asserted as **bytes rather than segments**: the test states that `weather.uk` matches `weather.uk-ish`, which is ZeroMQ's documented prefix semantics and the named loss 0007 records against weida's segmented filters, pinned here rather than described.
note: one test-shape disclosure from the worker, and it is the honest kind: the reconnect test rebinds the same port and retries the bind for up to 10 s, because closing a socket aborts its accept task and the kernel frees the port only once that task is really dropped — the retry is the operating system rather than a race in the library, and the comment in the test says so.

### B-079 — XPUB and XSUB
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done a8c0562 | needs: [B-078]
acceptance: XPUB delivers subscription messages to the application in the `1`/`0` form and synthesizes an unsubscribe when a subscriber disconnects; `ZMQ_XPUB_VERBOSE`, `_VERBOSER`, `_MANUAL`, `_WELCOME_MSG`; XSUB forwards subscriptions upstream and re-sends them on reconnect.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b079-zmq-xpub-xsub` by the parallel worker, off the B-078 tip dd585a4, with B-103 as a separate commit on the same branch — XSUB forwards subscriptions upstream over exactly the path B-103 bounds, so bounding it first is cheaper than bounding it under two socket types.
note: merged `--no-ff` as a8c0562 with B-103 and gated here: **636 tests**, one ignored; the crate's lib suite is 113. Two commits in the order asked for (f8d14db B-103, 5519b9d B-079), only `crates/zmq/weida-zmq/*`; the worker rewrote its branch after noticing its first attempt's second commit had swallowed the XPUB work, so the split is real rather than nominal and B-103's commit gates green on its own. `XPubSocket` and `XSubSocket` with the full endpoint surface, `SocketOptions::{xpub_verbose, xpub_verboser, xpub_manual, xpub_welcome_msg}` refused for every other socket type at construction, and `SocketCore::wait_for_activity`.
note: **the synthesized unsubscribe is the socket's memory, not the peer's**, and that is the design worth reading. A subscriber's table dies with its connection (B-073's rule that an accepted peer's queue goes with it), so XPUB cannot ask a departed peer what it held — it remembers per peer and turns a departure into one `%x00` per prefix. The memory is reconciled against the live peer set on every `recv`, so it is bounded by `max_peers` entries rather than growing with churn, which is B-076's routing-table answer applied a second time without being asked for.
note: the finding: **XPUB's `recv` cannot park on a receive.** A departure is an event XPUB owes its application, so `next_event` loops on `wait_for_activity` — which wakes on a message *or* a peer-set change — instead of awaiting `recv_fair`; the first version deadlocked exactly there and the departure test caught it. Smaller: `ZMQ_XPUB_MANUAL`'s `subscribe` applies to the peer whose subscription was delivered most recently, which is libzmq's "last pipe" rule stated rather than inferred, and with nothing delivered yet it is `EINVAL` rather than a silent no-op.

### B-103 — A subscription prefix is bounded in count but not in length
kind: code | size: 30 | status: done a8c0562 | needs: [B-078]
acceptance: a subscription prefix has a named, documented maximum length, refused past it the way an over-count subscribe already is, and `DEFAULT_MAX_SUBSCRIPTIONS`'s own product sentence is corrected to state both dimensions. A test subscribes a prefix one octet past the ceiling and asserts the refusal, and asserts that a prefix at the ceiling still filters correctly.
note: found on merging B-078, by reading its own documentation against its code. `DEFAULT_MAX_SUBSCRIPTIONS` says "37/ZMTP bounds a subscription neither in length **nor** in count" and then bounds only the count (`subscriptions.rs:104`): a peer may hold 1024 prefixes each as long as a `SUBSCRIBE` command body, which is `max_message_size` — 1 MiB by default. So the product the constant claims to bound is 1024 × 1 MiB per peer, times `max_peers`, not 1024 entries. B-054 bounded exactly these two dimensions in the bridge for exactly this reason and took 256 B per filter from [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §10; the library has the count half and owes the length half. Not a defect in the acceptance line of B-078, which never asked for it — a defect in the sentence that says what the ceiling covers.
note: merged in a8c0562, commit f8d14db, which gates green on its own. `DEFAULT_MAX_SUBSCRIPTION_BYTES = 256`, taken the way B-054 took it — [PROTOCOL.md](PROTOCOL.md) §10's filter bound — and cited in the constant's own documentation beside 37/ZMTP's `subscription = *OCTET`, which is the sentence that says why the number has to be ours. `Subscriptions::new(max, max_bytes)`; a prefix past the ceiling is refused exactly as an over-count subscribe is, counted in `refused()` with nothing stored, and `EINVAL` at the API for a socket's own `subscribe`. `SocketOptions` gains `max_subscription_bytes`, validated nonzero, and `subscription_limits()`. The product sentence now names both dimensions: 1024 × 256 B = **256 KiB per peer**, times `max_peers` (`subscriptions.rs:47-61`).

### B-080 — The inproc transport
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done ac5ec71 | needs: [B-072]
acceptance: a context-scoped namespace on `weida-runtime`'s registry with libzmq's 256-character budget, connect-before-bind working as libzmq 4.0 fixed it, and two contexts in one process never meeting.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b080-zmq-inproc` by the parallel worker, off the B-102 tip 8264e72 — B-102 was taken first, so this branch starts after it rather than off 5519b9d. The namespace is `weida-runtime`'s `NameRegistry`, which B-071 already put in the context under libzmq's 256-byte budget, so this item is the transport that uses it rather than the registry itself — and `transport.rs`'s single-variant `Stream` (B-073) is where the second variant finally arrives, so the `EPROTONOSUPPORT` that stands in for inproc today has to stop being returned at exactly the same time. The `Enumerated` guard of B-102 now means a socket type cannot reach a new transport without being on the list, which is one fewer thing this item has to remember.
note: merged `--no-ff` as ac5ec71 and gated here: **651 tests**, one ignored — 119 in the crate's lib suite, 8 in the new `tests/inproc.rs`, the doctest and the thread-rule harness. One commit (7aae6ef). New module `inproc.rs`: `Inproc` (the context's namespace — a `weida_runtime::NameRegistry<InprocDial>` plus one `Notify`), `InprocBinding`, `InprocDial` and `INPROC_BUFFER_BYTES` (64 KiB per connection, which is the bound this transport needed and it is named where it is taken). `Context::inproc()` now hands out `&Arc<Inproc>` rather than `&NameRegistry<…>`, because a binding outlives the call that made it. `Stream` gained its second arm at last, so B-073's honest single-variant enum is now a two-variant one and `check_transport` admits `inproc` — `ipc` alone still answers `EPROTONOSUPPORT`. `set_nodelay` is `Ok(())` there for the right reason, stated: there is no kernel to have an algorithm.
note: three decisions worth keeping. **Drop is the unbind:** the `InprocBinding` moves into the accept task, so `unbind` and `close` — which abort that task — release the name with no second piece of bookkeeping to forget, tested by unbinding and then re-binding the same name from another socket. **Connect-before-bind parks rather than polls,** and the ordering is the whole trick: `wait_until_bound` registers the `Notified` *before* it checks `is_bound`, which is the one race a wait like this can have, and the test binds only after the waiter has begun. **`ZMQ_CONNECT_TIMEOUT` is deliberately not applied to inproc,** with the reason in `dial`'s doc comment — there is no kernel, no address and no attempt to bound, so either the name is bound or the dial is early — while auto-reconnect *does* work, which is right per zeromq.md §13's note that 4.0 fixed both together: after a binder goes the connecter parks again and completes if the name is re-bound.
note: `EADDRINUSE` for a second bind of a live name, documented against §8's line that `ipc` "is not consistent with the `tcp` or `inproc` transports" precisely here — so B-081's endpoint-stealing hazard now has a counterpart in the tree to point at rather than a paragraph to write from scratch. `tests/inproc.rs` is one exchange per socket type plus the two inproc-specific claims, and its header says why it is not a copy of the pattern suites: no pattern knows which transport carried it, so the transport is what this file varies. One unasked-for correction folded in: the crate-doc "What remains" sentence had been left ungrammatical by B-079 and now names ipc, security and authorization properly.

### B-081 — The ipc transport
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done f27b926 | needs: [B-073]
acceptance: `AF_UNIX` with the hygiene of 0010 §4.5 — unlink-then-bind, explicit mode, socket-type check before unlinking, the 113-byte Linux path budget — peer credentials available to authorization, and the endpoint-stealing hazard documented rather than papered over.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b081-zmq-ipc` by the parallel worker, off the B-080 tip 7aae6ef. Everything this item needs on the OS side already exists and is tested: `weida-runtime`'s `BoundUnixSocket` (unlink-then-bind, socket-type check before unlinking, explicit mode, the `sun_path` budget, removal on drop) and `peer_credentials`, extracted in B-070 — so the work is the third `Stream` arm and the endpoint rules, not the hygiene. The stealing hazard is the one clause with no code behind it: B-080 has just documented `inproc`'s `EADDRINUSE` against §8's statement that `ipc` is *inconsistent* with tcp and inproc exactly there, so this item states what a second bind of a live path actually does on this platform and what an application can do about it, rather than describing the hazard in the abstract.
note: merged `--no-ff` as f27b926 and gated here: **664 tests**, one ignored — 127 in the lib suite, 8 inproc, 5 ipc integration tests, the doctest and the thread-rule harness. One commit (2b360da). `#[cfg(unix)] pub mod ipc` with `IpcBinding` and `dial` built on `weida-runtime`'s `BoundUnixSocket` and `peer_credentials`, so **nothing about the hygiene is reimplemented** — B-070's extraction paying off exactly as intended. `Stream` gained a `#[cfg(unix)] Unix { stream, principal }` arm whose constructor **captures the credentials and fails if the kernel will not answer**, because for `ipc` the kernel's answer is the only thing there is to know about the other end. `Peer` gained `credentials: Option<LocalPrincipal>` and every socket type gained `connections() -> Vec<Peer>`, which is what B-082's ZAP handler will read; a TCP peer's credentials are `None` and there is a test for it, rather than a zeroed principal that would read as root.
note: **two path budgets, kept because they answer different questions**, with the arithmetic in the module docs. The *endpoint string* is bounded at parse by libzmq's published 113 including the `ipc://` prefix, so a program ported from libzmq is refused at the same character and before anything touches the filesystem; the *path* is bounded at bind by the kernel's own `weida_core::MAX_SOCKET_PATH_BYTES` (107 Linux, 104 macOS — `sun_path` minus the terminator). On Linux they coincide, 107 + 6 = 113, "which is why libzmq's published number is really the kernel's"; on macOS the kernel's is stricter, so an endpoint libzmq's documented limit admits can still be refused at bind, with the platform's number in the message.
note: **the stealing hazard is asserted, not described**: `tests/ipc.rs`'s `a_second_bind_steals_the_endpoint` has the second bind succeed, a new connect reach the thief and the incumbent receive nothing new, and the doc comment says why refusing the bind would be worse — a socket file outlives its process, so a crash would leave the endpoint permanently unbindable, and an unlink cannot tell a stale node from a live one. The same code path has its own test as `a_stale_node_is_replaced`, which uses `mem::forget` to make the node a crash leaves. What is done and what is not is enumerated: only a *socket* is unlinked (anything else is `EADDRINUSE`, because a bind that deletes a caller's file is worse than one that fails), mode `0600` set after bind rather than inherited from `umask`, and **the directory named as load-bearing and as the thing this library cannot fix** (ipc.md §1.2, §7 — the substitution race is closed only by directory ownership and permissions).
note: one consequence the worker says it has not seen stated anywhere, and it is the kind of sentence this log exists for: **after a steal the node belongs to the thief, so whichever binding drops first unlinks it — the loser's unlink takes the winner's socket file.** libzmq unlinks on unbind too and has the same consequence. What the loser keeps is its accepted connections, because those are sockets rather than names. Credentials are also stated *as credentials and not as identity*, with the pointer that libzmq's own `ZMQ_IPC_FILTER_UID`/`_GID`/`_PID` did this filtering and are deprecated in favour of ZAP: the kernel fact belongs on the peer, the decision belongs to a handler.

### B-082 — PLAIN and the ZAP dialog
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 4142983 | needs: [B-074, B-080]
acceptance: PLAIN's handshake with username and password; RFC 27's request and reply framing over `inproc://zeromq.zap.01` with status 200/300/400/500 and the user-id field, one handler per context, `ZMQ_ZAP_DOMAIN` and `ZMQ_ZAP_ENFORCE_DOMAIN`; a 400 refuses the connection before any message flows, and the user id never becomes a weida identity.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b082-zmq-plain-zap` by the parallel worker, off the B-081 tip 2b360da. **This is the first item since B-072 whose commit touches `crates/adapters/weida-zmtp`**, because the PLAIN command layouts belong in the codec: the hard constraint is that `weida-zmtp`'s `[dependencies]` stays the empty section with its comment ([0013](decisions/0013-competitor-libraries.md) §4.3) — a byte layout needs nothing, and PLAIN's secret is carried, never verified, in that crate. Two things this item already has under it: `inproc://` exists (B-080), which is what RFC 27's handler endpoint is; and `Peer::credentials` exists (B-081), which is what a handler over `ipc://` has to decide on, stated there as a kernel fact rather than an identity.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 4142983 and gated here: **684 tests**, one ignored — `weida-zmq` 140 lib plus 8 inproc, 5 ipc, 5 plain_zap, the thread-rule harness and the doctest; `weida-zmtp` 45 lib plus 13 golden vectors (4 PLAIN rows new) plus 7 fuzz smoke. One commit (d0559e1). **`weida-zmtp`'s `[dependencies]` is still the empty section with its "not even weida-core" comment — read from the file here, and the worker says it checked with `cargo metadata` rather than by eye.** What the codec gained is octets only: `Command::Hello{username,password}`, `Welcome`, `Initiate(Metadata)`, `MAX_PLAIN_FIELD`, `CommandError::FieldTooLong`, four golden vectors, a foreign-HELLO decode and an extended fuzz bound check; zmtp.md §10.1 carries the rows with a paragraph saying they are 24/ZMTP-PLAIN's grammar rather than 37/ZMTP's. In `weida-zmq`: `Security` (Null/PlainServer/PlainClient) driving the greeting's mechanism and as-server octet, the handshake branching per mechanism, and a new `zap` module — `ZapRequest`/`ZapReply`/`ZapStatus`/`ZapUserId`/`AuthenticatedUser`, `ZAP_ENDPOINT`, the dialog over a **DEALER** socket because 27/ZAP allows "REQ or DEALER" and a DEALER adds nothing behind the caller's back.
note: **the bug, and it was found by two hanging tests rather than by review.** Stripping the envelope "up to the first empty frame" is wrong, because 27/ZAP's `identity` field is *legitimately empty* for a peer that announced none — so the scan mistakes a field for the delimiter. The **version frame is the anchor** instead: where `"1.0"` is first, there is no envelope. That single reader handles a REP-unwrapped request, a raw DEALER one and a ROUTER-routed one identically, and `zap::tests::an_empty_identity_is_not_a_delimiter` pins all three shapes. Its sibling asymmetry is stated in both module and method docs: `ZapRequest::encode` carries a delimiter and `ZapReply::encode` does not, because a REP handler's socket puts the request's envelope back itself and a reply carrying one would arrive doubled.
note: **the local principal reaches the handler as a named extension rather than as a faked address**, which is the right answer to a field 27/ZAP does not have: for `ipc` peers it travels as one trailing frame `X-Local-Principal: uid=…, gid=…, pid=…` after the credentials — additional, `X-`prefixed per 37/ZMTP's application convention, invisible to a handler that reads only the fields it knows, and never converted into a credential — while the address frame stays **empty** for local transports instead of carrying an invented IP. And `ZapUserId` has no bridge to any weida identity anywhere in the workspace (no `From`, `AsRef` or `Deref`), tested as a claim rather than asserted in prose: `LocalPrincipal` is a kernel fact, `Fingerprint` is a key, a user id is a string a handler chose (0013 §4.4 item 6).
note: two deviations for B-093's parity table, both because libzmq had no errno to borrow — it reports these only through monitor events. A refused peer is **`EACCES`**, which is not one of libzmq's socket errnos, and folding it into a protocol error would have said the peer was malformed when it was merely not allowed; a ZAP dialog that malfunctions (bad version, undefined status, mismatched request id) is `ENOCOMPATPROTO`, the `ZMQ_PROTOCOL_ERROR_ZAP_*` family having no errno either. Also documented at `zap::authorize`: each authorization spends one socket slot under `ZMQ_MAX_SOCKETS` for the duration of the handshake, exactly as libzmq's per-session ZAP socket does. And one note for whoever writes a handler, which is B-102's thread rule catching a real case: a handler must **bind before it is spawned**, because `bind(&self)` borrowed across a spawn makes the future not `Send`; both test harnesses do it that way and say so.

### B-083 — CURVE command layouts in weida-zmtp
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 07b11a7 | needs: [B-074]
acceptance: `HELLO` (200), `WELCOME` (168), `INITIATE` (257+), `READY` (30+) and `MESSAGE` encoded and decoded as byte layouts with the boxes as opaque ranges, the ABNF's 72-octet HELLO padding rather than the prose's 70, Z85 both ways, vectors published in zmtp.md and asserted both directions — and `weida-zmtp`'s `[dependencies]` still empty, because no crypto happens here.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: on branch `b083-zmtp-curve` by the parallel worker (session `zmq-lib`, which replaced `runtime-extract` after B-082), off the B-082 tip d0559e1. The empty-manifest clause is the whole point of splitting CURVE in two: **the layouts are octets and the crypto is B-084's**, so the boxes stay opaque ranges here and no RustCrypto dependency may appear in this commit. B-082 just proved the shape works — PLAIN's layouts landed in the codec with the manifest untouched — and left the instrument to reuse: zmtp.md §10.1 rows asserted both directions plus the fuzz smoke's bound check. Watch the ABNF's 72-octet HELLO padding against the prose's 70; the acceptance line takes the ABNF deliberately.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 07b11a7 and gated here: **710 tests**, one ignored. One commit (acd3c03), branched off main 4142983 rather than d0559e1 because B-082 was already merged. **`weida-zmtp`'s `[dependencies]` is still the empty section — read from the file here for the second item running**, which is the clause the whole CURVE split exists to keep. New: `src/curve.rs` (`CurveCommand` with HELLO 200, WELCOME 168, INITIATE 257+, READY 30+, MESSAGE 33+, every box a **borrowed opaque range**, the eight fixed nonce prefixes with `short_nonce`/`long_nonce`, `InitiatePlaintext` = C + vouch + metadata, and the cookie/vouch/welcome-plaintext split and join helpers) and `src/z85.rs` (`encode`/`decode` plus `encode_key`/`decode_key`, with 32/Z85's own `HelloWorld` vector both ways). `error.rs` gained `CurveError` and `Z85Error`; the fuzz smoke grew with them.
note: **the 72-versus-70 contradiction is decided by arithmetic and the losing reading is published as a row of its own.** 26/CURVEZMQ's ABNF says `hello-padding = 72%x00` and its prose says "This SHALL be 70 octets, all zero"; only 72 adds up to the specification's own 200-octet HELLO — 6 + 2 + 72 + 32 + 8 + 80 = 200, where 70 gives 198 — so the vectors use 72 in both directions and a 198-octet HELLO is **refused** with "CURVE HELLO is 200 octets, not 198". Refusing it is the behaviour that matters, because a reader that accepted it would take the signature box two octets out of phase. It is zmtp.md §10.1's third "disagrees with itself" bullet now, beside the length-prefixed command name and the `ERROR` reason that may contain spaces.
note: the doc half landed in this tree rather than on the branch, as instructed, and is committed separately as `1ead5bf` (`docs(zmtp): publish the CURVE layout vectors`): nine new §10.1 rows — six CURVE commands, the INITIATE plaintext, two Z85 — each asserted in both directions in `tests/golden_vectors.rs`, plus the paragraph that says **why a box can be published at all**: it is `crypto_box` output, so a vector containing one would be a vector of somebody's key material, and what can be checked against libzmq with a hex dump is where each box starts and how long it is. One thing I fixed while committing it: `[26/CURVEZMQ]`, `[32/Z85]` and B-082's `[24/ZMTP-PLAIN]` were written as bracketed reference links with no definition anywhere in the file, which renders as literal brackets — the file's own style writes `37/ZMTP` bare, so all three are bare now.

### B-084 — CURVE in weida-zmq
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 7f0bfa8 | needs: [B-083, B-082]
acceptance: the four keys; the cookie discarded on a valid INITIATE or after a short interval; session keys destroyed on close; the three security models selectable; `ZMQ_CURVE_SERVER`/`_PUBLICKEY`/`_SECRETKEY`/`_SERVERKEY` taking 32 bytes or 40-character Z85; the peer's long-term key reaching the ZAP handler as the CURVE credential; one named RustCrypto dependency and `unsafe_code` still forbidden.
note: from 0013 §5.3.
note: queued after B-083 on `b084-zmq-curve`. This is where the one named RustCrypto dependency arrives and where `unsafe_code = "forbid"` has to survive it, so the crate boundary matters: `weida-zmq` may take it, `weida-zmtp` may not. It also has its ZAP half ready — B-082's handler reads `ZapRequest.credentials`, so the peer's long-term key becomes the CURVE credential over the path that already exists, with `ZapUserId` still having no conversion to a weida identity.
note: on branch `b084-zmq-curve` by `zmq-lib`, off the B-083 tip acd3c03. Everything below it is now in place and none of it is crypto: the layouts and their nonce prefixes (B-083), the ZAP path a long-term key travels as a credential (B-082), and the option-validation shape that refuses what a socket type cannot honour (B-076's `validate_for`). So this item is exactly the sealing and opening, the key material's lifetime — the cookie discarded on a valid INITIATE or after a short interval, session keys destroyed on close — and the one named dependency. `unsafe_code = "forbid"` stays in the manifest, which is the constraint the dependency has to survive rather than a preference.
note: merged `--no-ff` as 7f0bfa8 and gated here: **725 tests**, one ignored. One commit (e3b06d0). `crates/zmq/weida-zmq/src/curve.rs`: `CurvePublicKey`/`CurveSecretKey` taking 32 octets or 40-character Z85, with a **redacting `Debug`, constant-time equality and a `Drop` that clears**; `keypair()`; `CurveClient` and `CurveServer` as the two handshake halves; `OpenedInitiate`; `CurveTransport`; `SecurityModel`/`security_model()`; `COOKIE_LIFETIME` 60 s. **The server keeps no state after its `WELCOME`** — C' and s' come back out of the cookie, whose key is dropped by a valid `INITIATE` or by the interval — which is 26/CURVEZMQ's own anti-amnesia design rather than a session table a stranger could grow. Session keys are destroyed through `Wire::destroy_session_keys` on **every** exit from the session loop, not only the clean one. Every frame after `READY` travels inside a `MESSAGE` box, commands included (libzmq's flags bit 1), which needed one constant — `MESSAGE_FLAG_COMMAND` — added to `weida-zmtp`'s curve module: octets only, and that manifest is **still the empty section**.
note: the dependency clause is met and the reasoning is in the manifest where a reader will hit it: **`crypto_box` 0.9, and nothing else** — `crypto_box::SalsaBox` *is* NaCl's `crypto_box` (X25519, HSalsa20, XSalsa20-Poly1305), which is the construction 26/CURVEZMQ is written in terms of, and it is pure Rust, so `unsafe_code = "forbid"` survived unchanged. `cargo tree -p weida-zmq --depth 1` here is `crypto_box`, `tokio`, `tracing`, `weida-core`, `weida-runtime`, `weida-zmtp` — no `weida`, no `weida-protocol`, one cryptographic dependency. Options: `curve_server`/`_publickey`/`_secretkey`/`_serverkey` with `Security::CurveServer`/`CurveClient`, and **every half-configured combination refused at `validate()` with `EINVAL` and a reason** — a server without a secret key, a client without its own pair, both roles at once, keys that select no mechanism, a half-right pair, CURVE beside PLAIN.
note: the handshake's own tests drive the two halves **in memory with no I/O at all**, which is the right instrument for a cryptographic state machine, and the wire-level CURVE tests drive the other side with this crate's client half over a raw socket against `CodecPeer` — octets rather than two copies of one state machine agreeing with each other. That choice came out of the thing the worker hit and worked around; see B-104, which is where it is recorded rather than left in a message.
