kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 3e905ec | needs: [B-158]
acceptance: links attached in both directions with a name unique among the links of that direction between the two containers, `source` and `target` termini carrying `address`, `durable`, `expiry-policy`, `timeout` and `dynamic`, handles allocated lowest-free with an `attach` on a handle in use answered by `close` carrying `amqp:session:handle-in-use`, `snd-settle-mode` and `rcv-settle-mode` negotiated, and an errored link endpoint detached with its error and its handle never silently reused; a second attach of the same link name steals it and the loser is closed with `amqp:link:stolen`.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b159-amqp-link
note: merged `--no-ff` as 3e905ec, one commit (21e21f6), no conflicts; gate green: **1437 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations and `-p weida-amqp --no-default-features`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `link.rs`, `terminus.rs` and `owned.rs`: attach and detach, both termini with their five fields, the **two independent handle spaces**, the steal answered with `amqp:link:stolen`, `handle-max` as the link table's bound and an errored handle **poisoned for the life of the session** rather than recycled — which is the clause most easily got wrong, because reuse is invisible until two links disagree about which one a `disposition` meant. Nine integration tests against a scripted server.
note: **one thing landed that the item did not name, and it is ungated: the commit also carries 2,824 lines of `crates/nats/weida-nats`** — error, options, message, request, subject, subscription and transport — while `[workspace] members` still lists only `weida-nats-codec`. So the NATS client is **in the tree and invisible to the gate**: `cargo test --workspace` never compiles it, and the 1437 tests above do not include a line of it. The worker's own note says the member line is held back in its worktree for B-165. I merged rather than rejecting the commit, because the AMQP half is the item and is complete, but this is recorded as the risk it is: **until B-165 lands with its member line, that directory is unproved code**, and the first thing B-165's merge must do is make the gate see it. If B-165 does not arrive, the directory is to be removed rather than left as a monument.
note: **closed, and closed the right way**: W4 chose to delete rather than to attach a member line, in `chore(nats): drop the ungated in-progress client` (merged here as eaa5c66, 2871 lines removed, gate green at 1456 tests with `crates/nats/` back to the codec alone). The reason it gave is the one that decides it: `cargo build -p weida-nats` had **never been run once** — nine files written, `tests/` empty, breakage expected in `connection.rs` — so a member line would have attached the gate to code that could not pass it, which is the same hole with a label on it. The work is not lost; it continues on `b165-nats-client` in a **separate worktree** with its own `CARGO_TARGET_DIR`, and that is also the fix for the root cause: the crate no longer exists in `../weida-w4`, so no `git add -A` can reach it and nobody has to toggle a member line in and out of the manifest around each gate run, which is what produced the hole. B-165 will arrive as one commit carrying the crate **and** its member line, gated together.

### B-160 — `transfer` and link credit
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done 4459034 | needs: [B-156, B-159]
acceptance: link credit counted in **messages** and granted only by the receiver through `flow`, with `delivery-count` and `link-credit` maintained at both ends, `drain` honoured and `echo` answered; a sender with no credit stalls instead of sending, transfers with `more` set reassemble into one delivery that never interleaves with another on the same link, `max-message-size` is refused before allocation, and the session's transfer window applies **on top of** the link's credit, because the two schemes are independent and simultaneously active.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b160-amqp-transfer-credit
note: merged `--no-ff` as 4459034, one commit (1449d5d), 2848 insertions against 44 deletions; gate green: **1599 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations and `-p weida-amqp --no-default-features`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `credit.rs` holds the four variables of Part 2 §2.6.7 with the absolute-limit arithmetic in RFC 1982 serial numbers and `drain`, 14 unit tests; `delivery.rs` holds `Delivery` and `Sent`; and the acceptance's hardest clause — **the two flow-control schemes are independent and simultaneously active** — is two tests rather than a sentence: a sender with **four units of link credit and a one-frame session window sends nothing** until a `flow` refreshes the window, and a 1600-octet message through a 512-octet frame costs **one unit of credit and four window units**. That is the pair I would have asked for.
note: **the bug its tests found is a scheduling bug with a wire consequence**: the driver's biased `select` preferred the command channel over the outbound queue, so `close` **overtook a transfer a sender had already completed** and the frame was dropped as having raced the close. "`close` MUST be the last frame ever written" makes it last; it is not a licence to discard what was queued before it — the Close arm now flushes the outbound queue first. A rule about ordering read as a rule about precedence is a mistake worth naming, because the code looked correct either way.
note: one deliberate choice where the specification offers two, recorded as a choice: **a delivery arriving past the credit this end had granted is handled and logged rather than answered with `amqp:link:transfer-limit-exceeded`**, because the sender sent under credit we granted and then withdrew, and dropping it would lose a message to our own race. I accept it — a receiver that punishes a peer for its own withdrawal is a receiver that loses messages on every credit reduction — and it belongs in B-163's parity table as a stated deviation. One codec addition rode along: `Reassembly::message_format()`, because the format arrives on the first frame and the frame that completes a delivery legitimately carries none. W4 also reports one full-workspace run printing "error: 1 target failed" with **no failing test and no failing result**, clean on three subsequent runs; it did not claim it away and neither do I — it matches the load-sensitive shape of B-180.

### B-161 — Dispositions, settlement and the unsettled map
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done dc86111 | needs: [B-160]
acceptance: an unsettled map at each end with settlement idempotent and one-way, `disposition` over ranges of delivery-ids carrying `accepted`, `rejected`, `released` or `modified`, and each settle-mode combination behaving as specified — settled on send, settled on disposition, and the `second` dance where the receiver waits for the sender's settlement; each test asserts what the mode *proves* to the application (the message is durable-or-not, the peer will or will not redeliver) rather than that a frame arrived.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b161-amqp-disposition
note: merged `--no-ff` as dc86111, one commit (1a53840), 2261 insertions against 9 deletions; gate green: **1641 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations and `-p weida-amqp --no-default-features`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `settlement.rs` holds the owned `Outcome` with **`received` deliberately excluded — it is a progress report, not an answer** — the delivery-id-keyed unsettled map with serial-arithmetic ranges, and 11 unit tests; the link gains `accept`/`reject`/`release`/`modify`, a `settle(first, last, outcome)` that covers a range in one frame, and `unsettled()`/`settled(id)`. Every one of the 13 integration tests asserts **what the exchange proves** rather than that a frame arrived: accepted and rejected do not come back, released and modified do, rejected and modified-with-delivery-failed count against a redelivery limit while accepted and released do not, and settled-on-send makes `settled(id)` an **error with the reason in the message**, because an application that could wait for that answer would wait forever.
note: three judgement calls, all three right and all three argued. **The `second` dance is driven by the driver rather than the application**: once a receiver publishes an outcome without settling, the sender owes a settling disposition and nothing is left to decide, so exposing a call would only invent a way to deadlock. **A settling disposition may carry no state at all** — it confirms what was already said — so `record_theirs` falls back to the outcome *this* end published; without that fallback the receiver's own `accepted` would be lost at the very moment it became final. And the **unsettled map is bounded only on the sender's side** (`DEFAULT_MAX_UNSETTLED`, 2048), with the module saying why: a receiver's map cannot grow except against credit it granted itself, so the protocol already bounds it, while a receiver that keeps granting credit and settles nothing would grow a *sender's* map without limit. That is the asymmetry argued from who controls the growth, which is the reasoning B-181 found missing elsewhere.

### B-162 — Interop against `fe2o3-amqp` and RabbitMQ
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 430f30a | needs: [B-161]
acceptance: sender and receiver roles against `fe2o3-amqp` as the pure-Rust peer that always runs, plus RabbitMQ 4.x — which speaks 1.0 natively on 5672 — under the process supervisor and `#[ignore]` with the reason where it is absent ([LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §2); the four things the sheet says implementations refuse are probed rather than assumed — `rcv-settle-mode=second`, transactions, link resumption and terminus expiry — and each outcome, including the address syntax each peer demanded, is recorded as measured against a named version.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b162-amqp-interop
note: merged `--no-ff` as 430f30a, one commit (5c2814e), 1740 insertions against 15 deletions; gate green: **1665 tests**, 0 failed, **17 ignored**, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations and `-p weida-amqp --no-default-features`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `fe2o3-amqp` 0.17 is a **dev-dependency with only its `acceptor` feature**, so the foreign peer always runs: 8 tests, nothing ignored, no broker and no C toolchain, and `cargo tree -p weida-amqp` still reaches neither `weida` nor `weida-protocol` and pulls no second TLS stack. RabbitMQ is genuinely absent here — `which rabbitmq-server`, docker and podman all empty — so its 4 tests are `#[ignore]`d behind one `needs_rabbitmq!()` macro carrying install commands for Arch, Debian and docker, with the harness starting and stopping the broker itself on its own port and waiting for the socket. **The module documentation says in those words that nothing in that file has been observed**, so B-163's table will record the RabbitMQ column as *not run* rather than as compatible. That is the distinction most interop documents lose.
note: **interop found three real bugs in this client, which is what interop is for.** (1) We **never answered a peer-initiated `detach`** — a link endpoint is destroyed only when both ends detach, and `fe2o3`'s `close()` does not return until the answer lands, so a peer whose application held the link waited forever; the fix answers with the peer's own `closed` flag and none of our error. (2) A `flow` for a handle whose link had just been cleanly detached ended the **session** with `amqp:session:unattached-handle` — that frame is a *race* with the detach, not a fault, and `fe2o3` really sends one; cleanly detached handles are now remembered and their frames discarded, while a handle detached **with an error** correctly draws `amqp:session:errant-link`, which Part 2 §2.6.5 names and which was not being distinguished at all. (3) A client that detached and immediately re-attached the same name **stole its own just-detached link**, sending `amqp:link:stolen` for a link the application itself closed and poisoning the handle. All three have deterministic scripted regression tests beside the interop ones, and one existing test was **strengthened** rather than adjusted: `an_errored_detach_poisons_its_handle` now reads the answering detach first, which asserts presence where it used to encode absence.
note: four measurements for B-163's table, each against **`fe2o3-amqp` 0.17.0 by name**: `rcv-settle-mode=second` is **honoured** by this peer, so the sheet's "implementations refuse it" is *not universal*; a terminus `expiry-policy=never` with `timeout=600` comes back **echoed rather than substituted**; a duplicate link name is **refused rather than stolen**, so this peer does not implement Part 2 §2.6.1's steal; and transactions are absent by construction on our side, because Part 4's target is a `coordinator` while `LinkOptions::target` is typed as a `Target` — the probe establishes the other half, that **no `amqp:txn:` capability is claimed by either end**. Four facts, each with a version attached, is what makes a parity row defensible.

### B-163 — The parity table against the OASIS core
kind: spec | size: 60 | status: done ff8031a | needs: [B-162]
acceptance: `docs/libraries/amqp.md` in the shape `docs/libraries/zmq.md` set — every performative, every message section, both credit schemes, the settle modes and delivery states, the SASL mechanisms and the transports, each present, refused-with-reason or absent-with-reason — with Part 4 transactions, link resumption and the three coexisting filter families named absent and the evidence for why, and the client/broker line of [0014](decisions/0014-parallel-libraries.md) stated once; no row says "partial" without naming what is missing.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b163-amqp-parity
note: merged `--no-ff` as ff8031a, one commit (6e3bb39): `docs/libraries/amqp.md` new at 301 lines, its index row, **and 38 lines of `link.rs`** — so this is not a docs-only item and the gate ran: **1668 tests**, 0 failed, 17 ignored, fmt, clippy in both workspace configurations and `-p weida-amqp --no-default-features`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. Counted here: **12 sections**, and the two occurrences of "partial" are both legitimate — one is the definition-of-done row quoting the rule, the other describes `received` as the state that "describes how far a **partial** body got". No row claims partial.
note: **the table found a claim the code had not implemented, which is exactly what a parity table is for.** `settlement.rs` *documented* that `send` waits at `DEFAULT_MAX_UNSETTLED`, and `take_credit` did not check it — the doc was written before the code was true. Fixed in this same commit: `take_credit` now takes the settled flag and requires room in the map for an **unsettled** delivery, since a settled one is recorded nowhere. That is the second time in this run that writing a document about the code corrected the code (B-093's option count was the first), and it is the argument for the parity table being a deliverable rather than a courtesy.
note: two rows I read closely because they are where such a table usually lies. **One row states what is *not* enforced rather than claiming presence**: Part 3 restricts application-properties *values* to simple types, this decoder checks only that the section is a map, so a nested value is carried rather than refused — the only "present with a hole" row in the document, and it names the hole. And the **transactions row is absent by construction rather than by refusal**: Part 4's target is a `coordinator`, a different described type, and `LinkOptions::target` is typed as a `Target`, so no value of the API can reach one — with the further fact that **Part 6, Distributed Transactions, was never published**, so one of the four capability names denotes nothing. The RabbitMQ column is recorded as **not run** in four places rather than as compatible, which is what B-162's unobserved file requires.

### B-164 — `weida-nats-codec`: the text protocol
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 9b9eb36 | needs: []
acceptance: `crates/nats/weida-nats-codec` with an **empty `[dependencies]`**: `INFO`, `CONNECT`, `PUB`, `HPUB`, `SUB`, `UNSUB`, `MSG`, `HMSG`, `PING`, `PONG`, `+OK` and `-ERR` encoded and decoded, CRLF-delimited with the declared byte count read before the payload and the payload never scanned for a terminator, the `NATS/1.0` header block with repeated names and preserved case, and a caller-supplied `max_payload` checked from the declared count before any allocation; a golden vector per verb and a fuzz target.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b164-nats-codec
note: merged `--no-ff` as 9b9eb36, one commit (f9b7f2a), no conflicts; gate green: **1400 tests**, 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. `cargo tree -p weida-nats-codec` prints exactly one node — the **fifth** codec in the tree with an empty `[dependencies]`, and the rule of 0013 §4.3 has now held for ZMTP, SP, MQTT, AMQP and NATS alike. All twelve verbs, 47 crate tests plus 8 golden vectors and 4 fuzz-smoke tests, with the fuzz crate its own workspace as the others are. **The payload is never scanned for a terminator**: the declared count comes off the control line, the body is taken by length, and the trailing CRLF is required at exactly the expected offset — with a test whose payload contains a CRLF in the middle, which is the test that makes the claim mean something.
note: three of this codec's bounds exist because **the protocol bounds nothing** there, and each is a caller argument on `Limits` checked from the declared count before any allocation: a header block declares no entry count at all (`a:\r\n` is four octets, so a 1 MiB block can ask for a quarter-million vector slots), a JSON array announces no length so `connect_urls` is capped while its elements are read, and `MAX_JSON_DEPTH` = 8 exists only so an unknown `INFO` field carrying an object can be **stepped over without recursing a peer into the stack**. The four `DEFAULT_*` values are documented as starting values rather than caps, which is the distinction that matters when the caller can raise them.
note: one published disagreement is recorded rather than silently resolved, which is the right instinct: `max_control_line` is documented as 1024 in the client protocol reference's error table and compiled as **4096** in `nats-server`. The crate names the larger and says why in the constant's doc comment — this bound applies to lines a *server* sends us, so the implementation's number is the one that decides whether we can read a real server at all. It belongs in `docs/libraries/nats.md` (B-169) and possibly in the sheet (B-174), and both items now have it waiting.

### B-165 — The client: `INFO`, `CONNECT`, TLS and the pings
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done fca6b72 | needs: [B-164]
acceptance: `crates/nats/weida-nats` on `weida-runtime`: the server's `INFO` read first, `CONNECT` answered with the selected capabilities and one of token, user and password, JWT, or an NKey signature over the server's nonce; TLS completed before ordinary traffic where `INFO` requires it; `PING`/`PONG` in both directions with the number of unanswered pings bounded rather than infinite; later asynchronous `INFO` handled outside the handshake, including `connect_urls` and the `ldm: true` drain notice; and the server's `max_payload` enforced locally so an oversized publish fails before the wire.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b165-nats-connection
note: merged `--no-ff` as fca6b72 — **one commit (6d70c00) carrying B-165, B-166 and B-167 together**, 7364 insertions over 16 files, and the workspace manifest is **in it**: `"crates/nats/weida-nats"` in `members` and `weida-nats` in `[workspace.dependencies]` land with the code, which is the rule this run set after the ungated-crate episode. Gate green here: **1573 tests** (1491 + 82), 0 failed, 1 ignored, fmt, clippy in both configurations, rustdoc with `-D warnings`; `weida-nats` itself is 40 lib + 18 handshake + 12 subscriptions + 10 request-reply + 2 doctests. `cargo tree -p weida-nats` reaches `weida-core`, `weida-nats-codec`, `weida-runtime`, `tokio`, `tokio-rustls` and **no `weida`, no `weida-protocol`**.
note: three decisions worth keeping. **NKey and JWT nonce signing is a caller-supplied closure rather than a dependency**, so the crate has *no cryptographic dependency at all* — the same rule `weida-amqp` applied to trust anchors, and the right one: key material is the application's. **There is no reconnect loop**, for the reason the sheet gives rather than by omission. And **`NoResponders` (the 503) stays distinct from `RequestTimeout`**, because "nobody is listening" and "nobody answered in time" are different facts about the system and collapsing them is how a client teaches its users to retry the wrong thing.

### B-166 — Subscriptions, subject matching and queue groups
kind: code | size: 90 | status: done fca6b72 | needs: [B-165]
acceptance: `SUB` with a client-chosen `sid` and an optional queue group, `UNSUB` with and without a message count, `MSG`/`HMSG` dispatched to the subscription that asked for it, and subject matching where `*` is exactly one token and `>` is one or more trailing tokens and must be last — `orders.*` matching `orders.created` and not `orders.eu.created`, `orders.>` matching both; two subscribers in one queue group receive one copy between them per publication while an ordinary subscriber beside them receives its own, which is the test that separates a group from a subject.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b166-nats-subscriptions

### B-167 — Request-reply over an inbox
kind: code | size: 60 | status: done fca6b72 | needs: [B-166]
acceptance: a request publishes with a reply-to subject under a unique `_INBOX.` prefix and the responder answers on it by ordinary subject interest with no hidden correlation field, the requester's timeout is its own and always bounded, and the scatter-gather form collects several responses inside one window; with `no_responders` and headers negotiated, a request with no responder returns that status immediately instead of waiting out the timeout, and the test asserts the difference between the two outcomes rather than only the happy path.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b167-nats-request-reply

### B-168 — Interop against `nats-server`
kind: adapter | size: 90 | status: done 248cd7c | needs: [B-167]
acceptance: publish, subscribe, queue groups and request-reply against `nats-server` started through the process supervisor with a `ready` condition and stopped in the same item on success and on failure alike, with an `async-nats` peer on the other side of that server for every case so the exchange is ours against an independent client rather than ours against ourselves; where the `nats-server` binary is absent the whole file is `#[ignore]` with the install command in its doc comment ([LOOP.md](LOOP.md) §2), and the item states plainly that there is no pure-Rust substitute — an `async-nats` peer is a client and needs the same server — so absence means no interop rather than a weaker one.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b168-nats-interop
note: merged `--no-ff` as 248cd7c, one commit (1ee3433), 862 insertions; gate green: **1668 tests**, 0 failed, **25 ignored**, fmt, clippy in the workspace configuration and `-p weida-nats --no-default-features`, rustdoc with `-D warnings`. **`nats-server` is not reachable on this machine and nothing in the file has been run** — I confirmed it here: `which nats-server` and `which nats` are both empty, and docker and podman are absent too — so all **8 tests are `#[ignore]`d** behind one `needs_nats_server!()` macro carrying four install routes, and the module documentation says so in the same words B-162's says about RabbitMQ. The item's own acceptance asked for that honesty and got it.
note: **the NATS-specific half of the honesty is the part worth keeping**, and it is stated where a reader will hit it: there is **no pure-Rust substitute**, so absence means *no interop at all* rather than a weaker one. `fe2o3-amqp` ships an `acceptor` — a foreign *server* — which is why AMQP interop runs anywhere; `async-nats` is a **client**, and two clients cannot talk to each other, because in Core NATS the whole of routing, queue-group selection and request-reply lives in the server. So `async-nats` is the independent peer on the far side of a real server and never a stand-in for one, and the documentation says explicitly that the handshake, subscription and request-reply suites are assertions about **the bytes this client writes**, checked against the protocol reference, and **not** evidence that a real server accepts them. Distinguishing "verified against a peer" from "verified against a document" is the distinction most interop chapters quietly lose.
note: what the eight would establish is written where each test is, and two of them are designed rather than assembled: the wildcard test's point is that **the server and this client's own `subject::matches` agree**, and the queue-group test holds one subscriber of *each* client with 20 jobs and asserts **the count rather than the split**, because a queue group is "one of the set" and not a load-balancing promise. The server would start on a readiness condition read off its own "Server is ready" line rather than a sleep, and stop in `Drop` so a panicking test leaves nothing behind. Two dependency notes, both checked: `async-nats` brings `tokio-rustls` and `tokio-websockets` which resolve to the **same rustls 0.23** already in the graph — no second TLS stack — and `cargo tree -p weida-nats --no-dev-dependencies` reaches neither `weida` nor `weida-protocol`; `futures-util` arrives as a dev-dependency for one reason, that `async-nats`'s `Subscriber` is a `Stream` while ours is an inherent method **on purpose**.

### B-169 — The parity table against Core NATS
kind: spec | size: 45 | status: done 78f35e2 | needs: [B-168]
acceptance: `docs/libraries/nats.md` in the shape `docs/libraries/zmq.md` set: every client verb, the two subject wildcards, queue groups, request-reply with inboxes, the authentication mechanisms and the transports, each present, refused-with-reason or absent-with-reason; JetStream, clusters, gateways and leaf nodes are named absent as server features with what a client would need to reach them, and the client/broker line of [0014](decisions/0014-parallel-libraries.md) is stated once.
note: workstream W4, worktree ../weida-w4, branch b169-nats-parity
note: merged `--no-ff` as 78f35e2, one commit (5a7dd6f): `docs/libraries/nats.md` new at 217 lines plus its index row, docs-only by the diff, and the code is unchanged since B-168's green gate at 1668 tests. **11 sections**, one occurrence of "partial" and it is not a row claiming it. **The thing to read first is §10: clause 4 of 0013 §4.7 is recorded as `NOT met`.** "Interop in both roles against an independent implementation" cannot be claimed — `nats-server` is absent and `async-nats` is a client, so there is no substitute — and rather than softening the clause or dropping it, the document says "NOT met, and stated rather than worked around" and names exactly what would change it: install the binary and run the `--ignored` suite. **That makes this the first library document in the repository with five of six**, and it is the honest number. §1 goes further and splits every claim into two kinds — read from the protocol reference and asserted against a scripted server that replays its byte sequences, or read from the code — saying in those words that the 40 unit plus 40 scripted tests are "assertions about the bytes this client writes and reads, not evidence that a real server accepts them".
note: two rows are judgement rather than transcription and both are right. **JetStream is absent but the row says it adds no verb** — it is `$JS.API` request/reply over the same twelve, with acknowledgements as payloads — so a caller learns the *shape* of what is missing rather than only its name. And **clusters are "absent as a feature, present as the one thing a client sees"**, because `INFO.connect_urls` is retained and readable and that is the whole client-visible surface of a cluster. The subject rules include the four degenerate forms **refused rather than normalised**, the 503 stays distinct from a timeout in the table as it is in the code, and the bounds table is marked the protocol's or ours line by line.
note: **this closes W4 — B-154..B-169, sixteen items: two codecs, two client libraries, two interop harnesses and two parity tables.** Its bindings (B-170..B-173) remain `ready` and depend only on `weida-py-core`, which is on main from W1's B-111.
